Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

This problem is same as the next one, but has smaller constraints.

Shiro's just moved to the new house. She wants to invite all friends of her to the house so they can play monopoly. However, her house is too small, so she can only invite one friend at a time.

For each of the $n$ days since the day Shiro moved to the new house, there will be exactly one cat coming to the Shiro's house. The cat coming in the $i$-th day has a ribbon with color $u_i$. Shiro wants to know the largest number $x$, such that if we consider the streak of the first $x$ days, it is possible to remove exactly one day from this streak so that every ribbon color that has appeared among the remaining $x - 1$ will have the same number of occurrences.

For example, consider the following sequence of $u_i$: $[2, 2, 1, 1, 5, 4, 4, 5]$. Then $x = 7$ makes a streak, since if we remove the leftmost $u_i = 5$, each ribbon color will appear exactly twice in the prefix of $x - 1$ days. Note that $x = 8$ doesn't form a streak, since you must remove exactly one day. 

Since Shiro is just a cat, she is not very good at counting and needs your help finding the longest streak.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $n$ ($1 \leq n \leq 10^5$) — the total number of days.

The second line contains $n$ integers $u_1, u_2, \ldots, u_n$ ($1 \leq u_i \leq 10$) — the colors of the ribbons the cats wear. 


-----Output-----

Print a single integer $x$ — the largest possible streak of days.


-----Examples-----
Input
13
1 1 1 2 2 2 3 3 3 4 4 4 5

Output
13
Input
5
10 2 5 4 1

Output
5
Input
1
10

Output
1
Input
7
3 2 1 1 4 5 1

Output
6
Input
6
1 1 1 2 2 2

Output
5


-----Note-----

In the first example, we can choose the longest streak of $13$ days, since upon removing the last day out of the streak, all of the remaining colors $1$, $2$, $3$, and $4$ will have the same number of occurrences of $3$. Note that the streak can also be $10$ days (by removing the $10$-th day from this streak) but we are interested in the longest streak.

In the fourth example, if we take the streak of the first $6$ days, we can remove the third day from this streak then all of the remaining colors $1$, $2$, $3$, $4$ and $5$ will occur exactly once.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the largest streak x such that if we remove exactly one day from the first x days, all remaining ribbon colors have the same frequency.

Key insights:
1. We can only remove exactly one day
2. After removal, all colors that appear must have the same count
3. We want the largest possible x

## Analysis of Valid Cases

Let's think about what happens when we remove one day:

**Case 1: All colors appear exactly once**
- If we remove one day, we'll have x-1 days with all colors appearing once
- This works for any x ≥ 2

**Case 2: One color appears twice, others appear once**
- If we remove one occurrence of the color that appears twice, all colors will appear once
- This works if we have exactly one color with count 2, and all others with count 1

**Case 3: All colors appear the same number of times**
- If we remove one day, we need to check if making one color have one fewer occurrence still keeps all others equal
- This only works if all colors have the same frequency except for one that has one more occurrence

**Case 4: More complex distributions**
- Generally, if we have multiple colors with different frequencies, we need to remove one day to balance them

## Algorithm Approach

For each possible streak length x (from 1 to n), we need to check if we can remove exactly one day to make all remaining colors have the same frequency.

For a given x, we:
1. Count frequencies of colors in the first x days
2. For each possible day to remove (i from 0 to x-1):
   - Simulate removing that day
   - Check if all remaining colors have the same frequency
3. If any removal works, x is a valid streak

However, this would be O(n²) which might be too slow for n=10⁵.

Let's optimize:

**Key observation**: After removing one day, all colors must have the same frequency. This means:
- Either all colors appear exactly k times (after removal)
- Or most colors appear k times, and one color appears k+1 times (which gets reduced to k)

So for a valid streak of length x:
- The total number of appearances is x (before removal)
- After removal: total appearances is x-1
- If all colors appear k times: k * (number of colors) = x-1
- If one color appears k+1 times and others appear k times: k * (c-1) + (k+1) = x-1 → k * c = x-1

In both cases, x-1 must be divisible by some k, and the frequencies must match.

## Optimized Algorithm

For each x from 1 to n:
1. Count frequency of each color in first x days
2. Check each possibility of removing one day:
   - Remove day i, update frequency
   - Check if all frequencies are equal
3. If any works, x is valid

Since we want the maximum x, we can iterate from n downwards and return the first valid x.

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    colors = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Try each possible streak length from n down to 1
    for x in range(n, 0, -1):
        # Count frequencies in first x days
        freq = {}
        for i in range(x):
            color = colors[i]
            freq[color] = freq.get(color, 0) + 1
        
        # Try removing each day
        valid = False
        for day in range(x):
            # Remove day 'day'
            color_to_remove = colors[day]
            new_freq = freq.copy()
            new_freq[color_to_remove] -= 1
            if new_freq[color_to_remove] == 0:
                del new_freq[color_to_remove]
            
            # Check if all remaining colors have same frequency
            if len(new_freq) == 0:
                # Only one color was present, after removal it's gone
                valid = True
                break
                
            # Get the frequency of the first color
            target_freq = list(new_freq.values())[0]
            
            # Check if all frequencies match target_freq
            all_same = True
            for count in new_freq.values():
                if count != target_freq:
                    all_same = False
                    break
            
            if all_same:
                valid = True
                break
        
        if valid:
            return x
    
    return 1

print(solve())
```

Wait, let me reconsider the complexity. For n=10⁵, checking each x from n down to 1 and for each x checking x possibilities (removing each day) would be O(n²) = 10¹⁰ operations in worst case, which is too slow.

We need a more efficient approach.

Let me think again. Actually, the constraints say u_i is between 1 and 10, which is very small! This means there are at most 10 distinct colors.

So I can optimize by noting that the number of distinct colors is small (≤ 10).

For a streak of length x to be valid:
- After removing one day, all colors that appear have the same frequency
- This frequency must divide (x-1) if we have c colors

But still, the direct simulation might be acceptable if we optimize the inner loop.

Let me implement the straightforward solution with the constraint that colors are only 1-10:


View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the largest streak `x` such that if we remove exactly one day from the first `x` days, all remaining ribbon colors have the same frequency.

Key observations:
1. We can only remove exactly one day
2. After removal, all remaining colors must have the same count
3. We want the maximum possible `x`

## Analysis of Valid Cases

Let's think about when a streak can be valid after removing exactly one day:

**Case 1: All colors appear exactly once**
- If all colors appear once in the first `x` days, removing any one day will make all remaining colors appear exactly once (if we remove a color that appeared once).
- This works for any valid removal.

**Case 2: Only one color appears more than once**
- If one color appears `k` times and all others appear once, removing one occurrence of that color makes all colors appear exactly once.
- This works.

**Case 3: Two colors appear more than once**
- If we have two colors with frequencies `f1` and `f2`, we need to remove one day to make all frequencies equal.
- This is possible only if `f1 - 1 = f2 - 1 = f3 = ... = f_k` (all others appear once).
- Or if `f1 = f2` and we remove one occurrence from one of them to make both `f1-1 = f2-1`.

**Case 4: All colors appear the same number of times**
- If all colors appear `k` times, removing one occurrence of any color makes that color appear `k-1` times while others appear `k` times. This doesn't work unless `k = 1` (which is Case 1).

Wait, let me reconsider Case 4:
- If all colors appear `k` times, removing one occurrence of a color that appears `k` times leaves that color with `k-1` occurrences and others with `k` occurrences. 
- For all to be equal, we need `k-1 = k`, which is impossible.

## Correct Analysis

After removing one day, all remaining colors must have the same frequency. This means:

1. If we start with frequencies `f1, f2, ..., fm`, after removing one day:
   - The frequency of that day's color becomes `fi-1`
   - All other frequencies remain the same

2. For all to be equal: `fi-1 = fj` for all j ≠ i, and `fi-1 = fj` for all j

3. This means either:
   - All colors except one have frequency `k`, and one color has frequency `k+1`
   - All colors have frequency `k`, and we remove one occurrence of one color (which would make that color `k-1` while others are `k`) - this doesn't work unless `k = 0` or `k = 1`
   
Actually, for all to be equal after removal:
- If a color had frequency `f`, after removing one occurrence it becomes `f-1`
- All other colors remain at their original frequency
- So we need: `f-1 = g` for all other colors `g`

This means either:
1. All colors except one have frequency `k`, and one color has frequency `k+1`
2. All colors have frequency `1` (so removing any one makes all colors have frequency `1`, but actually makes one color have frequency `0`)

Wait, if a color has frequency `1`, removing it makes it have frequency `0`. So colors with frequency `1` become `0` after removal.

For all remaining colors to have the same frequency, the frequency must be the same for all colors that still exist.

Cases:
- Case A: All colors appear exactly once initially. After removing one color, all remaining colors appear exactly once.
- Case B: One color appears more than once, all others appear once. After removing one occurrence of the frequent color, all colors appear once.
- Case C: Two colors appear more than once with frequencies f1 and f2, we remove one occurrence from one of them to make them equal to other colors.

Case C: If freq1 = f, freq2 = f, then removing one from either makes both f-1, so if all other colors have frequency f-1, it works.

So general pattern:
- We have frequencies: f1, f2, ..., fk
- After removing one day with color c, color c has frequency f_c - 1, others have their original frequencies
- For equality: f_c - 1 = f_j for all j ≠ c

This means all non-c colors have the same frequency `f`, and color c has frequency `f+1`.

Also, if all colors have frequency `f`, then after removal we have one color with `f-1` and others with `f`, which are not equal unless f-1 = f (impossible).

## Algorithm

For each possible streak length x from n down to 1:
1. Count frequencies of colors in first x days
2. For each color, try removing one occurrence of that color
3. Check if after removal, all remaining colors have the same frequency

Return the largest x where this is possible.

But we want efficiency - with n up to 10^5, we can't try all x and for each try all colors.

Alternative approach:
Since colors are only from 1 to 10, we can be smarter.

We can iterate through each possible ending position, and for each position, check if we can remove exactly one day.

Actually, let me implement the direct simulation with optimization:

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    colors = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Try each possible streak length from n down to 1
    for x in range(n, 0, -1):
        freq = {}
        # Count frequencies in first x days
        for i in range(x):
            freq[colors[i]] = freq.get(colors[i], 0) + 1
        
        # Try removing each day one by one
        valid = False
        for color in freq:
            # Try removing one occurrence of this color
            new_freq = freq.copy()
            new_freq[color] -= 1
            if new_freq[color] == 0:
                del new_freq[color]
            
            # Check if all remaining frequencies are equal
            values = list(new_freq.values())
            if len(values) == 0:
                # Only one color originally, removing it leaves nothing, which is valid (no colors to compare)
                valid = True
                break
            elif len(set(values)) == 1:
                valid = True
                break
        
        if valid:
            print(x)
            return
    
    # Should not reach here
    print(1)

solve()
```

Wait, let me check the examples:

Example 1: [1,1,1,2,2,2,3,3,3,4,4,4,5], x=13
- After removal of 5 (last element), we have frequencies: 1:3, 2:3, 3:3, 4:3
- All are 3, so this works.

Example 4: [3,2,1,1,4,5,1], answer is 6
- First 6 days: [3,2,1,1,4,5]
- Frequencies: 3:1, 2:1, 1:2, 4:1, 5:1
- Try removing 3: 2:1, 1:2, 4:1, 5:1 → values {1,2} → not equal
- Try removing 2: 3:1, 1:2, 4:1, 5:1 → values {1,2} → not equal  
- Try removing first 1: 3:1, 2:1, 1:1, 4:1, 5:1 → values all 1 → works!

So this should work.

But is it efficient? O(n²) in worst case, with n=10^5, that might be too slow.

We can optimize by precomputing frequencies and breaking early.

Actually, since colors are only 1-10, we can also check the pattern directly.

But let me first implement the straightforward version and see if it passes.

```python
n = int(input())
colors = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Try each possible streak length from n down to 1

    freq = {}
    # Count frequencies in first x days

    # Try removing each color that appears in the streak
    valid = False
    for color in freq:
        # Try removing one occurrence of this color
        new_freq = freq.copy()
        new_freq[color] -= 1
        if new_freq[color] == 0:
            del new_freq[color]
        
        # Check if all remaining frequencies are equal
        values = list(new_freq.values())
        if len(values) == 0:
            valid = True
            break
        if len(set(values)) == 1:
            valid = True
            break
    
    if valid:
        print(x)
        break
```

This should work. Let me verify with the examples.

Example 5: [1,1,1,2,2,2], answer is 5
- x=6: frequencies {1:3, 2:3}. Try removing 1: {1:2, 2:3} → not equal. Try removing 2: {1:3, 2:2} → not equal. So x=6 fails.
- x=5: frequencies {1:3, 2:2}. Try removing 1: {1:2, 2:2} → all equal to 2 → valid. Answer is 5. Correct.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the largest streak `x` such that if we remove exactly one day from the first `x` days, all remaining ribbon colors appear the same number of times.

Key observations:
1. We remove exactly one day, so we have `x-1` days left
2. After removal, all colors that appear should have the same frequency
3. We want the maximum possible `x`

## Analysis

Let's think about what happens when we remove one day:

**Case 1: All colors appear exactly once after removal**
- This means originally, all colors appeared exactly once, except one color that appeared twice (the one we remove)
- So the original frequencies: one color has frequency 2, all others have frequency 1

**Case 2: All colors appear `k` times after removal**
- This means originally, each color has frequency `k` or `k+1`
- If we remove a day of color `c`, then color `c` goes from `k+1` to `k`, and all others stay at `k`

So the possible scenarios are:
1. All colors appear exactly once after removal (original: one color appears twice, others once)
2. All colors appear `k` times after removal (original: most colors appear `k` times, one color appears `k+1` times)

## Algorithm

For each possible streak length `x` from 1 to `n`, check if we can remove one day to make all remaining colors have the same frequency.

But since we want the maximum `x`, we can work backwards or use a more efficient approach.

Actually, we can check for each position `i` (from 0 to n-1), whether removing the `i`-th day results in all colors having the same frequency.

Steps:
1. For each possible removal position, count frequencies of colors in the remaining days
2. Check if all frequencies are equal
3. Find the maximum streak length where this is possible

However, this would be O(n²). Let's optimize.

## Optimized Approach

For a streak of length `x`, we need to check if there exists a day we can remove such that all remaining colors have the same frequency.

Let's go through the examples to understand:

Example 4: [3,2,1,1,4,5,1], answer is 6
- If we remove day 3 (0-indexed): [2,1,1,4,5,1] → frequencies: 2:1, 1:3, 4:1, 5:1 → not equal
- If we remove day 4 (0-indexed): [3,2,1,1,5,1] → frequencies: 3:1, 2:1, 1:3, 5:1 → not equal  
- If we remove day 5 (0-indexed): [3,2,1,1,4,1] → frequencies: 3:1, 2:1, 1:3, 4:1 → not equal
- If we remove day 2 (0-indexed): [3,2,2,4,5,1] → frequencies: 3:1, 2:2, 4:1, 5:1, 1:1 → not equal
- If we remove day 1 (0-indexed): [3,1,1,4,5,1] → frequencies: 3:1, 1:3, 4:1, 5:1 → not equal
- If we remove day 0 (0-indexed): [2,1,1,4,5,1] → frequencies: 2:1, 1:3, 4:1, 5:1 → not equal

Wait, let me reconsider. The answer is 6, so we consider the first 6 days: [3,2,1,1,4,5]

Check removal:
- Remove day 0 (3): [2,1,1,4,5] → 2:1, 1:2, 4:1, 5:1 → not equal
- Remove day 1 (2): [3,1,1,4,5] → 3:1, 1:2, 4:1, 5:1 → not equal  
- Remove day 2 (1): [3,2,1,4,5] → 3:1, 2:1, 1:1, 4:1, 5:1 → all 1! ✓

Ah, I see. The frequencies are all 1 after removing day 2.

## Solution Strategy

For each possible streak length `x` from `n` down to 1, check if there exists a day to remove such that all remaining colors have the same frequency.

We can do this efficiently:

1. For a fixed streak length `x`, try removing each day from 0 to `x-1`
2. For each removal, count frequencies of remaining colors
3. Check if all frequencies are equal
4. Return the maximum `x` for which this is possible

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    u = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Try each possible streak length from n down to 1
    for x in range(n, 0, -1):
        # Check if we can remove one day from first x days to make all colors have same frequency
        found = False
        
        # Try removing each day from 0 to x-1
        for remove_idx in range(x):
            # Create frequencies of colors after removing remove_idx
            freq = {}
            for i in range(x):
                if i == remove_idx:
                    continue
                color = u[i]
                freq[color] = freq.get(color, 0) + 1
            
            # Check if all frequencies are equal
            if freq:
                frequencies = list(freq.values())
                if len(set(frequencies)) == 1:
                    found = True
                    break
        
        if found:
            return x
    
    return 1

print(solve())
```

Wait, let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: [1,1,1,2,2,2,3,3,3,4,4,4,5], answer is 13
- For x=13, we need to check if removing any day makes all frequencies equal
- If we remove the last day (5): colors 1,2,3,4 each appear 3 times → all equal ✓

Example 4: [3,2,1,1,4,5,1], answer is 6
- For x=6: first 6 days [3,2,1,1,4,5]
- Remove day 2 (value 1): remaining [3,2,1,4,5] → frequencies: 3:1, 2:1, 1:1, 4:1, 5:1 → all 1 ✓

This seems correct.

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

This problem is same as the next one, but has smaller constraints.

Shiro's just moved to the new house. She wants to invite all friends of her to the house so they can play monopoly. However, her house is too small, so she can only invite one friend at a time.

For each of the $n$ days since the day Shiro moved to the new house, there will be exactly one cat coming to the Shiro's house. The cat coming in the $i$-th day has a ribbon with color $u_i$. Shiro wants to know the largest number $x$, such that if we consider the streak of the first $x$ days, it is possible to remove exactly one day from this streak so that every ribbon color that has appeared among the remaining $x - 1$ will have the same number of occurrences.

For example, consider the following sequence of $u_i$: $[2, 2, 1, 1, 5, 4, 4, 5]$. Then $x = 7$ makes a streak, since if we remove the leftmost $u_i = 5$, each ribbon color will appear exactly twice in the prefix of $x - 1$ days. Note that $x = 8$ doesn't form a streak, since you must remove exactly one day. 

Since Shiro is just a cat, she is not very good at counting and needs your help finding the longest streak.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $n$ ($1 \leq n \leq 10^5$) — the total number of days.

The second line contains $n$ integers $u_1, u_2, \ldots, u_n$ ($1 \leq u_i \leq 10$) — the colors of the ribbons the cats wear. 


-----Output-----

Print a single integer $x$ — the largest possible streak of days.


-----Examples-----
Input
13
1 1 1 2 2 2 3 3 3 4 4 4 5

Output
13
Input
5
10 2 5 4 1

Output
5
Input
1
10

Output
1
Input
7
3 2 1 1 4 5 1

Output
6
Input
6
1 1 1 2 2 2

Output
5


-----Note-----

In the first example, we can choose the longest streak of $13$ days, since upon removing the last day out of the streak, all of the remaining colors $1$, $2$, $3$, and $4$ will have the same number of occurrences of $3$. Note that the streak can also be $10$ days (by removing the $10$-th day from this streak) but we are interested in the longest streak.

In the fourth example, if we take the streak of the first $6$ days, we can remove the third day from this streak then all of the remaining colors $1$, $2$, $3$, $4$ and $5$ will occur exactly once.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the largest streak `x` such that if we remove exactly one day from the first `x` days, all remaining ribbon colors have the same frequency.

Key observations:
1. We can remove exactly one day (not zero or more than one)
2. After removal, all colors that appear should have the same count
3. We want the maximum possible `x`

## Analysis of Cases

Let's think about when we can satisfy the condition after removing one day:

**Case 1: All colors appear exactly once**
- If we remove one day, all remaining colors appear exactly once → this works
- This happens when all elements in the prefix are distinct

**Case 2: One color appears more times than others**
- If we remove one occurrence of the most frequent color, all other colors should have the same frequency
- This means the frequencies of all colors except the most frequent one should be equal
- The most frequent color had frequency `f`, after removal it becomes `f-1`
- All other colors have frequency `g`
- We need `f-1 = g`

**Case 3: All colors have the same frequency**
- If we remove one day, the most frequent color decreases by 1, so we need that decrease to make all frequencies equal
- This means the original frequency was 1, and after removal all others are 0 (but we only remove one occurrence)

## Algorithm

For each possible streak length `x` from `n` down to 1:
1. Count frequencies of colors in the first `x` days
2. Try removing each day one by one
3. Check if after removal, all remaining colors have the same frequency

But we need to be efficient since `n` can be up to 10^5.

Alternative approach:
Since constraints are small (colors from 1 to 10), we can use the frequency properties:

For a prefix of length `x` to work:
- Remove one occurrence of color `c`
- After removal, all colors should have the same frequency
- This means: all frequencies should be equal, except possibly one frequency that is reduced by 1

So we can:
1. For each prefix length `x`, get the frequency count
2. For each color that appears, try removing one occurrence of that color
3. Check if all remaining frequencies are equal

But we want the maximum `x`.

## Implementation Strategy

Since we want the maximum streak, we can iterate from `n` down to 1 and return the first valid `x`.

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    colors = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    def works(x):
        if x == 0:
            return True
        if x == 1:
            return True  # removing the only day leaves nothing, all colors have same frequency (0)
            
        # Count frequencies in first x days
        freq = {}
        for i in range(x):
            color = colors[i]
            freq[color] = freq.get(color, 0) + 1
        
        # Try removing each color occurrence
        for color in freq:
            # Create new frequency without one occurrence of this color
            new_freq = freq.copy()
            new_freq[color] -= 1
            if new_freq[color] == 0:
                del new_freq[color]
            
            # Check if all remaining frequencies are equal
            if len(new_freq) == 0:
                return True
                
            frequencies = list(new_freq.values())
            if all(f == frequencies[0] for f in frequencies):
                return True
        
        return False
    
    # Check from n down to 1 to find the maximum streak
    for x in range(n, 0, -1):
        if works(x):
            return x
    
    return 1

print(solve())
```

Example 4: `[3, 2, 1, 1, 4, 5, 1]` → expected output 6
- For x=6: colors = [3,2,1,1,4,5]
- Frequencies: 3:1, 2:1, 1:2, 4:1, 5:1
- Remove one 1: frequencies become 3:1, 2:1, 1:1, 4:1, 5:1 → all have frequency 1 → valid!

This works. Let me optimize a bit for performance:

```python
def works(x):
    if x == 0:
        return True
    if x == 1:
        return True
        
    # Count frequencies in first x days
    freq = {}
    for i in range(x):
        color = colors[i]
        freq[color] = freq.get(color, 0) + 1
    
    # Try removing each color occurrence
    for color in list(freq.keys()):
        # Create new frequency without one occurrence of this color
        new_freq = freq.copy()
        new_freq[color] -= 1
        if new_freq[color] == 0:
            del new_freq[color]
        
        # Check if all remaining frequencies are equal
        if len(new_freq) == 0:
            return True
            
        frequencies = list(new_freq.values())
        if all(f == frequencies[0] for f in frequencies):
            return True
    
    return False

# Check from n down to 1 to find the maximum streak
for x in range(n, 0, -1):
    if works(x):
        print(x)
        break
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the largest streak `x` such that if we remove exactly one day from the first `x` days, all remaining ribbon colors have the same frequency.

Key observations:
1. We remove exactly one day (not zero or more than one)
2. After removal, all remaining colors should have the same count
3. We want the maximum possible `x`

## Analysis of Possible Cases

After removing one day, all colors should have the same frequency. Let's think about what happens:

**Case 1: All colors appear exactly once after removal**
- This means originally, we had all colors appearing once, plus one extra occurrence of some color
- When we remove one occurrence of that color, all colors have frequency 1

**Case 2: All colors appear k times after removal**
- This means originally, we had some colors appearing k times, and one color appearing k+1 times
- When we remove one occurrence of that color, all colors have frequency k

**Case 3: Only one color exists after removal**
- This means we had one color counting 2 times, and we remove one occurrence, leaving it with 1 occurrence
- This is actually covered in Case 2 with k=1

## Algorithm Steps

For each possible streak length from 1 to n, we need to check if removing exactly one day makes all remaining colors have the same frequency.

However, we can optimize this. Instead of checking every streak, we can:

1. For each position i (0 to n-1), simulate removing the i-th day and check if the resulting frequency distribution is uniform
2. Return the maximum streak length where this is true

For a given prefix of length x, when we remove day i (0-indexed), we get:
- All colors except the one at position i
- The frequency of the color at position i is reduced by 1

We need to check if all remaining colors have the same frequency.

## Implementation Strategy

For each prefix length x from 1 to n:
- Create the array of first x elements
- For each position i in [0, x-1]:
  - Remove element at position i
  - Count frequencies of remaining elements
  - Check if all frequencies are equal
- If any removal makes frequencies equal, x is a valid streak

We want the maximum such x.

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    colors = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    def can_form_streak(prefix_length):
        # Check if we can remove exactly one day from first prefix_length days
        # to make all remaining colors have same frequency
        
        # If prefix_length is 1, we remove the only day, leaving 0 days
        # But we need at least one color to have frequency, so this only works if we have 0 colors
        # Actually, after removal of one day, we have 0 days, which is valid (vacuously true)
        # But the problem states "every ribbon color that has appeared" - if no colors, it's true
        if prefix_length == 1:
            return True
            
        prefix = colors[:prefix_length]
        
        # Try removing each day
        for remove_pos in range(prefix_length):
            # Create frequency map of remaining elements
            freq = {}
            for i in range(prefix_length):
                if i != remove_pos:
                    color = prefix[i]
                    freq[color] = freq.get(color, 0) + 1
            
            # Check if all frequencies are equal
            if len(freq) == 0:
                # No colors after removal - valid (vacuously true)
                return True
            elif len(freq) == 1:
                # Only one color remains - valid
                return True
            else:
                # Multiple colors, check if all have same frequency
                values = list(freq.values())
                if all(v == values[0] for v in values):
                    return True
                    
        return False
    
    # Find the maximum streak
    result = 1
    for x in range(1, n + 1):
        if can_form_streak(x):
            result = x
    
    print(result)

solve()
```

Wait, let me verify this with the examples:

Example 4: [3,2,1,1,4,5,1], expected output 6
- For x=6: [3,2,1,1,4,5]
- Remove position 2 (value 1): [3,2,1,4,5] - frequencies: {3:1, 2:1, 1:1, 4:1, 5:1} - all 1, valid
- So x=6 works

Example 5: [1,1,1,2,2,2], expected output 5
- For x=5: [1,1,1,2,2]
- Remove position 0: [1,1,2,2] - frequencies {1:2, 2:2} - valid
- So x=5 works

The algorithm seems correct, but let me optimize it since n can be up to 10^5.

The current approach is O(n²) which might be acceptable for n=10^5 in PyPy/C++ but might be borderline in Python. However, given the small color constraint (colors are 1-10), we can optimize further.

Actually, let me check the constraints again. Colors are between 1 and 10, so only 10 possible colors. This means we can optimize the frequency counting.

But the current solution should work since the only interesting cases are when we remove one element. Let me finalize:

```python
            return True
        elif len(freq) == 1:
            return True

            frequencies = list(freq.values())
            if all(f == frequencies[0] for f in frequencies):
                return True
                
    return False
```

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the largest number x such that in the first x days, we can remove exactly one day so that all remaining ribbon colors have the same frequency.

Key observations:
1. We remove exactly one day from the first x days
2. After removal, all remaining colors should have the same count
3. We want the maximum possible x

## Analysis

Let's think about when we can remove one day to make all remaining colors have the same frequency:

Case 1: All colors appear exactly once
- If we remove one day, all remaining colors appear exactly once
- This works for any x ≥ 1

Case 2: One color appears more frequently than others
- If we remove one occurrence of the most frequent color, all colors become equally frequent
- This works when the most frequent color appears exactly one more time than others

Case 3: Two colors have the same frequency, and the rest are less
- If we remove one occurrence of one of the colors, we make all colors equal
- This works when we have exactly two colors with frequency f, and others with frequency f-1

Actually, let me reconsider. After removing one day, all remaining colors should have the same frequency.

Let me formalize:
- After removing one day, let the frequency of each color be f
- Before removing, one color had frequency f+1, and others had frequency f
- So we need exactly one color that appears one more time than all others

Or:
- All colors appear exactly f times after removal
- So before removal, all colors appear f times except one that appears f+1 times

Wait, there's another case:
- All colors appear exactly once after removal, so before removal, one color appears twice and others appear once

So the conditions are:
1. All colors appear exactly once after removal → before removal, one color appears twice, others appear once
2. All colors appear exactly f times after removal → before removal, one color appears f+1 times, others appear f times

## Algorithm

For each possible x from 1 to n, check if we can remove exactly one day to make all remaining colors have the same frequency.

But we want the maximum x, so we can check from n down to 1.

For each x, we need to:
1. Count frequency of each color in first x days
2. For each possible day to remove (from 0 to x-1), check if after removal all colors have the same frequency

However, this is O(n²) which might be too slow for n=10⁵.

Let me think of a better approach:

For a valid streak of length x:
- Let the final frequency of each color be f
- The sum of frequencies is x-1
- So (x-1) = sum of f over all colors

Actually, since all colors have the same frequency f after removal:
- Total elements = (number of colors) × f = x-1

So f = (x-1) / (number of colors)

For this to work, (x-1) must be divisible by the number of distinct colors.

But there's a constraint: we can only remove one day.

So:
- If we remove a day with color c, then:
  - The frequency of c decreases by 1
  - All other frequencies stay the same

For the result to have equal frequencies:
- All colors except c should have frequency f
- Color c should have frequency f-1 (if it was f+1 before removal)

So:
1. Count frequencies of all colors in first x days
2. For each color c that appears in the first x days:
   - If we remove one occurrence of c, then all colors should have the same frequency
   - This means: for all colors d ≠ c, freq[d] = f, and freq[c] = f-1
   - And f = (x-1) / (number of colors)

Wait, I'm making this too complex.

Let me simplify:
- After removing one day, all colors have the same frequency f
- So the total sum of frequencies after removal is (x-1) = num_colors × f
- Therefore f = (x-1) / num_colors
- This means (x-1) must be divisible by the number of distinct colors

For each possible x from n down to 1:
1. Count frequencies of colors in first x days
2. Let k = number of distinct colors
3. If (x-1) % k == 0, then f = (x-1) // k
4. Check if we can remove exactly one day to make all colors have frequency f

How to check? For each color that appears in the first x days:
- Remove one occurrence of this color
- The new frequency of this color is freq[c] - 1
- For all other colors, frequency remains the same

This should equal f for all colors.

So we need:
- For color c: freq[c] - 1 == f
- For all other colors d: freq[d] == f

This means:
- Exactly one color has frequency f+1
- All other colors have frequency f
- And (f+1) + k×f = x
- So x = f×k + 1

This also means that (x-1) = f×k, which is consistent.

So the algorithm:
For each x from n down to 1:
1. Count frequency of colors in first x days
2. Let k = number of distinct colors
3. If (x-1) % k == 0:
   - f = (x-1) // k
   - Count how many colors have frequency exactly f+1
   - If exactly one color has frequency f+1, then x is valid
4. Return the first valid x (which will be the largest)

Let me test with examples:

Example 1: [1,1,1,2,2,2,3,3,3,4,4,4,5], x=13
- x=13, k=5 (colors 1,2,3,4,5)
- x-1=12, 12%5 = 2 ≠ 0 → not divisible, skip
Wait, colors are 1,2,3,4,5 → 5 colors
12 % 5 = 2 ≠ 0

But the answer is 13. Let me check the example again.

Actually, looking at the example: "upon removing the last day out of the streak, all of the remaining colors 1, 2, 3, and 4 will have the same number of occurrences of 3"

So after removing the last day (color 5), we have:
- color 1: 3 times
- color 2: 3 times  
- color 3: 3 times
- color 4: 3 times
- color 5: 0 times (removed)

So colors 1,2,3,4 have frequency 3 each, and color 5 has frequency 0.

So we only care about colors that appear at least once after removal.

Actually, the problem says "every ribbon color that has appeared" - so only colors that appear after removal.

But after removal, color 5 doesn't appear.

So the check should be: after removal, all colors that appear have the same frequency.

For x=13, removing day 13 (color 5):
- Colors present: 1,2,3,4
- Frequencies: 3,3,3,3 → all same → valid

So for a valid x:
- After removing one day, for every color that appears in the remaining sequence, its frequency is the same

This means:
- After removal, all non-zero frequencies are equal to some value f
- The sum of frequencies is x-1
- The number of colors that appear after removal is some k', and k' × f = x-1

But we can also have cases where some colors become 0 (removed).

So the algorithm:
For each x from n down to 1:
1. Count frequencies of colors in first x days
2. For each possible color c that appears in the first x days:
   - Simulate removing one occurrence of color c
   - Count how many colors have frequency > 0 and all have the same frequency
   - If yes, x is valid
3. Return x

But this is O(n²) in worst case.

Since n ≤ 10⁵, O(n²) might be too slow.

We need a more efficient check.

Alternative approach:
For a valid x, after removing one occurrence of color c:
- The frequencies should be: all colors have frequency f, except c has frequency freq[c]-1
- This means if we let f be the target frequency, then:
  - For all colors d: freq[d] = f or freq[d] = f+1
  - Specifically: exactly one color has frequency f+1 (color c), and all others have frequency f

So:
- All frequencies should be either f or f+1
- Number of colors with frequency f+1 should be exactly 1
- And sum of frequencies = (number of colors with f) × f + (number of colors with f+1) × (f+1) = (k-1) × f + 1 × (f+1) = k×f + 1 = x

So x = k×f + 1

We can iterate over possible f and k to check.

Actually, since the frequencies are small (colors from 1 to 10), we can compute the frequencies efficiently.

Given constraints (colors 1 to 10), we can do:

For each x from n down to 1:
1. Count frequencies for colors in first x days
2. For each color c that has freq > 0:
   - Create new frequency array by decreasing freq[c] by 1
   - Count how many colors have freq > 0
   - If all positive frequencies are equal, x is valid
3. Return first valid x

Since there are at most 10 colors, and we iterate over at most 10 possibilities, this is O(10×n) = O(n), which is acceptable.

```python
n = int(input())
colors = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Try each x from n down to 1
for x in range(n, 0, -1):
    # Count frequencies of colors in first x days
    freq = [0] * 11  # colors from 1 to 10
    for i in range(x):
        freq[colors[i]] += 1
    
    # Check if we can remove exactly one day
    valid = False
    for c in range(1, 11):  # each color from 1 to 10
        if freq[c] > 0:  # color c appears in first x days
            # Simulate removing one occurrence of color c
            new_freq = freq[:]
            new_freq[c] -= 1
            
            # Check if after removal, all colors that appear have the same frequency
            if new_freq[c] == 0:  # color c is completely removed
                # Only colors with freq > 0 should have same frequency
                target_freq = None
                all_same = True
                for color in range(1, 11):
                    if new_freq[color] > 0:
                        if target_freq is None:
                            target_freq = new_freq[color]
                        elif new_freq[color] != target_freq:
                            all_same = False
                            break
                if all_same:
                    valid = True
                    break
            else:  # color c still appears
                # All colors that appear should have the same frequency
                target_freq = new_freq[c]
                all_same = True
                for color in range(1, 11):
                    if new_freq[color] > 0:
                        if new_freq[color] != target_freq:
                            all_same = False
                            break
                if all_same:
                    valid = True
                    break
    
    if valid:
        print(x)
        break
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

This problem is same as the next one, but has smaller constraints.

Shiro's just moved to the new house. She wants to invite all friends of her to the house so they can play monopoly. However, her house is too small, so she can only invite one friend at a time.

For each of the $n$ days since the day Shiro moved to the new house, there will be exactly one cat coming to the Shiro's house. The cat coming in the $i$-th day has a ribbon with color $u_i$. Shiro wants to know the largest number $x$, such that if we consider the streak of the first $x$ days, it is possible to remove exactly one day from this streak so that every ribbon color that has appeared among the remaining $x - 1$ will have the same number of occurrences.

For example, consider the following sequence of $u_i$: $[2, 2, 1, 1, 5, 4, 4, 5]$. Then $x = 7$ makes a streak, since if we remove the leftmost $u_i = 5$, each ribbon color will appear exactly twice in the prefix of $x - 1$ days. Note that $x = 8$ doesn't form a streak, since you must remove exactly one day. 

Since Shiro is just a cat, she is not very good at counting and needs your help finding the longest streak.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $n$ ($1 \leq n \leq 10^5$) — the total number of days.

The second line contains $n$ integers $u_1, u_2, \ldots, u_n$ ($1 \leq u_i \leq 10$) — the colors of the ribbons the cats wear. 


-----Output-----

Print a single integer $x$ — the largest possible streak of days.


-----Examples-----
Input
13
1 1 1 2 2 2 3 3 3 4 4 4 5

Output
13
Input
5
10 2 5 4 1

Output
5
Input
1
10

Output
1
Input
7
3 2 1 1 4 5 1

Output
6
Input
6
1 1 1 2 2 2

Output
5


-----Note-----

In the first example, we can choose the longest streak of $13$ days, since upon removing the last day out of the streak, all of the remaining colors $1$, $2$, $3$, and $4$ will have the same number of occurrences of $3$. Note that the streak can also be $10$ days (by removing the $10$-th day from this streak) but we are interested in the longest streak.

In the fourth example, if we take the streak of the first $6$ days, we can remove the third day from this streak then all of the remaining colors $1$, $2$, $3$, $4$ and $5$ will occur exactly once.

Let me verify with the first example: `[1,1,1,2,2,2,3,3,3,4,4,4,5]`, x=13

So the condition is: either
- There's a color with frequency f+1 and all others with frequency f (for some f), OR
- There's a color with frequency 1, and after removal all remaining colors have the same frequency.

For the second case, after removing a color with frequency 1, the remaining colors must all have the same frequency.

For the first case (f+1 and f), after removing one occurrence from the color with frequency f+1, we get frequency f for all colors.

So I need to check both cases for each x.

Actually, I can simplify: 
For a prefix to be valid, after removing exactly one day, all colors that remain have the same frequency.

So for each color we could remove:
- If we remove a color that appears exactly once: check if all other colors have the same frequency
- If we remove a color that appears k times (k >= 2): check if after removal, all colors have the same frequency

For k >= 2, after removal, the frequencies become: one color has k-1, others unchanged. For all to be equal, we need that all colors have the same frequency after removal.

This happens only if:
- Before removal, one color had frequency f+1, and all others had frequency f (so after removal, all have frequency f)

So overall, for a prefix of length x to be valid:
1. There exists a color c with frequency 1, and all other colors have the same frequency
2. OR there exists a color c with frequency f+1, and all other colors have frequency f

I'll implement this.

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
colors = list(map(int, input().split()))

def is_valid_streak(prefix_length):
    if prefix_length == 0:
        return False
    if prefix_length == 1:
        return True  # Only one day, remove it, no colors left (or one color with freq 0) - consider valid
    
    # Count frequencies of colors in first prefix_length days
    freq = {}
    for i in range(prefix_length):
        c = colors[i]
        freq[c] = freq.get(c, 0) + 1
    
    # Case 1: Remove a color that appears exactly once
    # After removal, all remaining colors should have the same frequency
    if 1 in freq.values():
        # For each color with frequency 1, try removing it
        for c in list(freq.keys()):
            if freq[c] == 1:
                # Remove color c
                remaining_freq = freq.copy()
                del remaining_freq[c]
                
                # Check if all remaining colors have the same frequency
                if len(remaining_freq) == 0:
                    return True  # After removal, no colors - valid
                if len(set(remaining_freq.values())) == 1:
                    return True
    
    # Case 2: Remove one occurrence from a color that appears multiple times
    # After removal, all colors should have the same frequency
    # This happens if one color had frequency f+1 and all others had frequency f
    max_freq = max(freq.values())
    count_freq = {}
    for f in freq.values():
        count_freq[f] = count_freq.get(f, 0) + 1
    
    # Check if there's exactly one color with max_freq, and all others have frequency max_freq-1
    if max_freq > 0:
        if count_freq.get(max_freq, 0) == 1:
            # Check if all others have frequency max_freq-1
            others_freq = []
            for c in freq.keys():
                if freq[c] != max_freq:
                    if freq[c] != max_freq - 1:
                        break
                    others_freq.append(freq[c])
            else:
                # All non-max_freq colors have frequency max_freq-1
                if len(others_freq) == len(freq) - 1:  # all non-max_freq colors
                    # But we need to verify all non-max_freq are max_freq-1
                    if all(f == max_freq - 1 for f in freq.values() if f != max_freq):
                        return True
    
    return False

# Find the maximum valid streak
ans = 0
for x in range(1, n + 1):
    if is_valid_streak(x):
        ans = x

print(ans)
```

Wait, there's an issue with the second case. I think the logic is off.

Let me redefine:

For the second case: we remove one occurrence from a color with frequency f+1, and we want all colors to have frequency f after removal.

This requires:
1. There is exactly one color with frequency f+1
2. All other colors have frequency f

So in the frequency map, there should be:
- Exactly one color with frequency f+1
- All other colors with frequency f

For the first case: we remove a color with frequency 1, and we want all remaining colors to have the same frequency.

This requires:
- There is exactly one color with frequency 1
- All other colors have the same frequency (say f)

I need to fix the code.

Also, when we remove a color with frequency 1, the remaining colors should all have the same frequency.

```python
n = int(input())
colors = list(map(int, input().split()))

def is_valid_streak(prefix_length):
    if prefix_length == 0:
        return False
    if prefix_length == 1:
        return True  # Remove the only day - no colors. Consider valid
    
    # Count frequencies of colors in first prefix_length days
    freq = {}
    for i in range(prefix_length):
        c = colors[i]
        freq[c] = freq.get(c, 0) + 1
    
    # Case 1: Remove a color that appears exactly once
    # After removal, all remaining colors should have the same frequency
    for c in list(freq.keys()):
        if freq[c] == 1:
            # Remove color c
            remaining_freq = {}
            for color, count in freq.items():
                if color != c:
                    remaining_freq[color] = count
            
            # If no colors left, it's valid
            if len(remaining_freq) == 0:
                return True
            
            # Check if all remaining colors have the same frequency
            if len(set(remaining_freq.values())) == 1:
                return True
    
    # Case 2: Remove one occurrence from a color that appears multiple times
    # After removal, all colors should have the same frequency
    # This happens if: one color had frequency f+1, and all others had frequency f
    max_freq = max(freq.values()) if freq else 0
    
    # Check if exactly one color has frequency max_freq, and all others have frequency max_freq-1
    if max_freq > 0:
        # Count how many colors have max_freq
        count_max = sum(1 for f in freq.values() if f == max_freq)
        count_max_minus_1 = sum(1 for f in freq.values() if f == max_freq - 1)
        
        # Need exactly one color with max_freq, and all others with max_freq-1
        if count_max == 1 and count_max_minus_1 == len(freq) - 1:
            # Also need to ensure max_freq - 1 >= 1, or handle zero frequency
            # For any color with frequency max_freq-1, if max_freq-1 == 0, it would be zero
            # But if a color has frequency 0, it's not in freq
            # So for max_freq-1 >= 1, or if max_freq-1 == 0, then there are no colors with max_freq-1
            # Actually, if max_freq-1 == 0, then no colors have frequency 0, which is fine
            # But we need all non-max_freq colors to have frequency max_freq-1
            # So if max_freq == 1, then max_freq-1 == 0, so we need no colors with frequency 0
            # This means after removal, we would have all colors with frequency 0, which means no colors
            # But if max_freq == 1, and we remove from a color that has frequency 1, then after removal all have 0
            # And that's not what we want for the second case
            # Actually, if max_freq == 1, then all colors have frequency 1, so count_max-1 should be 0
            # But if max_freq == 1, then max_freq-1 == 0, and we can't have colors with frequency 0
            # So we need to be careful
            
            # Actually, if max_freq == 1, then all colors have frequency 1
            # So count_max = number of colors with frequency 1 = all colors
            # So we need count_max == 1, which means only one color, so n=1, which we handled separately
            
            # So we need max_freq >= 2 for this case
            if max_freq >= 2:
                # Verify that there is exactly one color with max_freq and all others with max_freq-1
                # We've already checked count_max == 1 and count_max_minus_1 == len(freq) - 1
                return True
    
    return False

# Find the maximum valid streak
```

Wait, I think I'm making this too complicated. Let me check the example again.

For `[1,1,1,2,2,2,3,3,3,4,4,4,5]`, x=13:
- freq: {1:3, 2:3, 3:3, 4:3, 5:1}
- Case 1: color 5 has frequency 1, remove it. Remaining: 1:3, 2:3, 3:3, 4:3 - all have frequency 3. Valid!

So it should work.

But in my second case, I'm checking for max_freq >= 2, which is not needed here.

The second case is: remove one occurrence from a color with frequency f+1, making it f, and all others have frequency f.

In the example, no color has frequency f+1 with others having f - all have frequency 3 except one with 1.

So only case 1 works.

Let me fix the second case: we want to remove one occurrence from a color with frequency f+1, such that after removal, all colors have frequency f.

This requires:
- Exactly one color has frequency f+1
- All other colors have frequency f

So in the frequency map, there is one color with frequency f+1, and all others have frequency f.

This can be checked as:
- max_freq = f+1
- count_max = 1
- all other frequencies are f = max_freq - 1

I'll implement this correctly:

```python
n = int(input())
colors = list(map(int, input().split()))

def is_valid_streak(prefix_length):
    if prefix_length == 1:
        return True
    
    # Count frequencies of colors in first prefix_length days
    freq = {}
    for i in range(prefix_length):
        c = colors[i]
        freq[c] = freq.get(c, 0) + 1
    
    # Case 1: Remove a color that appears exactly once
    # After removal, all remaining colors should have the same frequency
    for c in list(freq.keys()):
        if freq[c] == 1:
            # Remove color c
            remaining_freq = {}
            for color, count in freq.items():
                if color != c:
                    remaining_freq[color] = count
            
            # If no colors left, it's valid
            if len(remaining_freq) == 0:
                return True
            
            # Check if all remaining colors have the same frequency
            if len(set(remaining_freq.values())) == 1:
                return True
    
    # Case 2: Remove one occurrence from a color that appears multiple times
    # After removal, all colors should have the same frequency
    # This happens if: one color had frequency f+1, all others had frequency f
    if len(freq) >= 2:  # at least two colors
        max_freq = max(freq.values())
        # Count how many colors have max_freq
        count_max = sum(1 for f in freq.values() if f == max_freq)
        
        # Check if exactly one color has max_freq and all others have max_freq-1
        if count_max == 1:
            # All other colors should have frequency max_freq - 1
            all_have_max_minus_1 = True
            for f in freq.values():
                if f != max_freq and f != max_freq - 1:
                    all_have_max_minus_1 = False
                    break
            if all_have_max_minus_1:
                return True
    
    return False

# Find the maximum valid streak
ans = 0
for x in range(1, n + 1):
    if is_valid_streak(x):
        ans = x

print(ans)
```

```python
        return True

                return True
            
            # Check if all remaining colors have the same frequency
            if len(set(remaining_freq.values())) == 1:
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

This problem is same as the next one, but has smaller constraints.

Shiro's just moved to the new house. She wants to invite all friends of her to the house so they can play monopoly. However, her house is too small, so she can only invite one friend at a time.

For each of the $n$ days since the day Shiro moved to the new house, there will be exactly one cat coming to the Shiro's house. The cat coming in the $i$-th day has a ribbon with color $u_i$. Shiro wants to know the largest number $x$, such that if we consider the streak of the first $x$ days, it is possible to remove exactly one day from this streak so that every ribbon color that has appeared among the remaining $x - 1$ will have the same number of occurrences.

For example, consider the following sequence of $u_i$: $[2, 2, 1, 1, 5, 4, 4, 5]$. Then $x = 7$ makes a streak, since if we remove the leftmost $u_i = 5$, each ribbon color will appear exactly twice in the prefix of $x - 1$ days. Note that $x = 8$ doesn't form a streak, since you must remove exactly one day. 

Since Shiro is just a cat, she is not very good at counting and needs your help finding the longest streak.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $n$ ($1 \leq n \leq 10^5$) — the total number of days.

The second line contains $n$ integers $u_1, u_2, \ldots, u_n$ ($1 \leq u_i \leq 10$) — the colors of the ribbons the cats wear. 


-----Output-----

Print a single integer $x$ — the largest possible streak of days.


-----Examples-----
Input
13
1 1 1 2 2 2 3 3 3 4 4 4 5

Output
13
Input
5
10 2 5 4 1

Output
5
Input
1
10

Output
1
Input
7
3 2 1 1 4 5 1

Output
6
Input
6
1 1 1 2 2 2

Output
5


-----Note-----

In the first example, we can choose the longest streak of $13$ days, since upon removing the last day out of the streak, all of the remaining colors $1$, $2$, $3$, and $4$ will have the same number of occurrences of $3$. Note that the streak can also be $10$ days (by removing the $10$-th day from this streak) but we are interested in the longest streak.

In the fourth example, if we take the streak of the first $6$ days, we can remove the third day from this streak then all of the remaining colors $1$, $2$, $3$, $4$ and $5$ will occur exactly once.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the largest streak `x` such that if we remove exactly one day from the first `x` days, all remaining ribbon colors have the same frequency.

Key observations:
1. We can only remove exactly one day from the streak
2. After removal, all remaining colors must have the same count
3. We want the longest possible streak

## Analysis of Valid Cases

When we remove one day, we need all remaining colors to have the same frequency. Let's think about what scenarios are possible:

### Case 1: All colors appear exactly once
- Remove any day, all remaining colors appear once (same frequency)
- This works for any streak

### Case 2: All colors appear k times except one color that appears k+1 times
- Remove one occurrence of the color that appears k+1 times
- All colors now appear exactly k times
- This works

### Case 3: All colors appear k times except one color that appears k-1 times
- This can't work since removing one day won't make the frequencies equal

### Case 4: Multiple colors have different frequencies
- This doesn't work as removing one day won't balance all frequencies

## Algorithm Approach

For each possible streak length from n down to 1, we check if it's valid:
1. Count frequency of each color in the first x days
2. Try removing each day and check if all remaining colors have the same frequency

However, this would be O(n²) which might be too slow for n=10⁵.

Let's think of a more efficient approach:

For a streak of length x to be valid:
- Either all colors appear the same number of times (except possibly one color that appears one more time)
- Or after removing one day, all colors appear the same number of times

Actually, let me reconsider the mathematical properties:

After removing one day:
- If we have k distinct colors, each must appear the same number of times
- Let the frequency of colors be f₁, f₂, ..., f_k
- After removing one day of color c, the frequencies become f₁, f₂, ..., f_c-1, f_{c+1}, ...
- For this to work, all remaining frequencies must be equal

So:
1. Either all frequencies are equal (remove any day)
2. Exactly one frequency is one more than the others (remove one occurrence of that color)

Actually, let me simplify:

For a streak of length x to be valid:
- Let f be the frequency of the most common color
- If all colors have frequency f, then removing any day makes the most common color have frequency f-1, and others have frequency f → not equal
- Wait, that's wrong.

Let me reconsider with examples:

If all colors have frequency k:
- Remove one occurrence of a color → that color has frequency k-1, others have frequency k → not equal

If one color has frequency k+1, others have frequency k:
- Remove one occurrence of the color with frequency k+1 → all colors have frequency k → valid

If all colors have frequency k except one with frequency k-1:
- Remove one occurrence of the color with frequency k-1 → that color has frequency k-2, others have frequency k → not equal

So the only valid cases are:
1. All colors appear k times, and we remove one of them → makes them all appear k-1 times? No, that doesn't work.
2. Exactly one color appears one more time than the others (say k+1 times), others appear k times. Remove one occurrence of the (k+1) color → all appear k times.

Actually, if all colors appear k times, and we remove one occurrence, the removed color will have k-1, others have k → not equal.

So only valid case is: one color appears one more time than all others, and we remove one occurrence of that color.

But what if all colors appear the same frequency? Then removing any day breaks the symmetry.

Actually, let me check the examples:

Example 1: [1,1,1,2,2,2,3,3,3,4,4,4,5]
- All appear 3 times except 5 appears 1 time
- If we remove 5, then all colors appear 3 times → valid

Example 4: [3,2,1,1,4,5,1] → first 6 days: [3,2,1,1,4,5]
- Frequencies: 3:1, 2:1, 1:2, 4:1, 5:1
- Remove third day (1): now frequencies: 3:1, 2:1, 1:1, 4:1, 5:1 → all 1 → valid

So the valid cases are:
1. All colors appear k times, and we remove one occurrence of any color → but then the removed color has k-1, others have k → not equal unless k=1
2. Exactly one color appears one more time than others, and we remove one occurrence of that color → all become equal
3. All colors appear exactly once → remove any day → all others appear once

Case 3: All appear once. After removing one, the remaining colors all appear once → valid.

Case 2: One color appears more than others. After removing one occurrence of the overrepresented color, all have equal frequency.

So algorithm:
For each x from n down to 1:
1. Count frequencies of colors in first x days
2. Check if it's valid by trying to remove one day
   
But this is O(n²) and n can be 10⁵.

We need to optimize. Let's think about the constraints.

The colors are from 1 to 10, so only 10 possible colors.

For a streak of length x to be valid:
- Either all colors appear exactly once (then removing any day works)
- Or one color appears one more time than all others (then removing one occurrence of that color makes them all equal)

Actually, let's define:
- Let freq[c] be the frequency of color c
- Let max_freq = max(freq.values())
- Let count_max = number of colors with frequency = max_freq

If max_freq == 1 and count_max == 10: all appear once → valid (remove any day)
If max_freq - min_freq == 1 and count_max == 9: one color appears one more than others → valid (remove one occurrence of the max color)
If max_freq - min_freq == 0: all same frequency → only valid if all are 1 (since removing any day makes some have frequency 0)

Wait, if all frequencies are equal to k, and k > 1, then removing one day makes some have k-1, others have k → not equal.

If all frequencies are equal to 1, removing any day makes remaining colors have frequency 1 → valid.

So valid cases:
1. All colors appear exactly once → valid
2. Exactly one color appears one more time than all others → valid

Let me implement this with optimization:

For each possible x from n down to 1:
- Count frequencies in first x days
- Check if valid using the above conditions
- Return first valid x (since we want largest)

```python
n = int(input())
colors = list(map(int, input().split()))

def is_valid(x):
    if x == 0:
        return False
    if x == 1:
        return True
    
    # Count frequencies of colors in first x days
    freq = {}
    for i in range(x):
        c = colors[i]
        freq[c] = freq.get(c, 0) + 1
    
    # Get all frequencies
    frequencies = list(freq.values())
    
    # Case 1: All colors appear exactly once
    if len(frequencies) == len(set(frequencies)) and all(f == 1 for f in frequencies):
        return True
    
    # Case 2: Exactly one color appears one more time than others (all others appear same frequency)
    if len(frequencies) == len(set(frequencies)) and len(set(frequencies)) > 1:
        # Find max frequency
        max_freq = max(frequencies)
        # Count how many colors have max_freq
        count_max = sum(1 for f in frequencies if f == max_freq)
        
        # Check if all colors have frequency either max_freq or (max_freq-1)
        if max_freq == 1:
            # All appear once, already handled above
            return False
            
        # Check if max_freq - min_freq == 1
        min_freq = min(frequencies)
        if max_freq - min_freq == 1:
            # Only one color has max_freq, others have min_freq
            if count_max == 1:
                return True
    
    # Edge case: all frequencies equal
    if len(set(frequencies)) == 1:
        # All appear same frequency f
        # After removing one day, one color has f-1, others have f
        # For this to be same frequency, we need f-1 == f which is impossible
        # Unless f == 1
        if frequencies[0] == 1:
            return True
    
    return False

# Check from n down to 1
for x in range(n, 0, -1):
    if is_valid(x):
        print(x)
        break
```

Wait, I think I'm overcomplicating. Let me simplify and fix:

```python
n = int(input())
colors = list(map(int, input().split()))

def is_valid(x):
    if x == 1:
        return True
    
    # Count frequencies of colors in first x days
    freq = {}
    for i in range(x):
        c = colors[i]
        freq[c] = freq.get(c, 0) + 1
    
    freqs = list(freq.values())
    
    # Case 1: All colors appear exactly once
    if all(f == 1 for f in freqs):
        return True
    
    # Case 2: One color appears one more than others
    max_freq = max(freqs)
    min_freq = min(freqs)
    
    # If max_freq is exactly 1 more than min_freq, and only one color has max_freq
    if max_freq == min_freq + 1:
        count_max = sum(1 for f in freqs if f == max_freq)
        if count_max == 1:
            return True
    
    # Case 3: All frequencies are equal (only when equal to 1)
    if len(set(freqs)) == 1 and freqs[0] == 1:
        return True
    
    return False

# Check from n down to 1
for x in range(n, 0, -1):
    if is_valid(x):
        print(x)
        break
```

n = int(input())
colors = list(map(int, input().split()))

def is_valid(x):
    if x == 1:
        return True
    
    # Count frequencies of colors in first x days
    freq = {}
    for i in range(x):
        c = colors[i]
        freq[c] = freq.get(c, 0) + 1
    
    freqs = list(freq.values())
    
    # Case 1: All colors appear exactly once
    if all(f == 1 for f in freqs):
        return True
    
    # Case 2: One color appears one more than others
    max_freq = max(freqs)
    min_freq = min(freqs)
    
    # If max_freq is exactly 1 more than min_freq, and only one color has max_freq
    if max_freq == min_freq + 1:
        count_max = sum(1 for f in freqs if f == max_freq)
        if count_max == 1:
            return True
    
    # Case 3: All frequencies are equal (only when equal to 1)
    if len(set(freqs)) == 1 and freqs[0] == 1:
        return True
    
    return False

# Check from n down to 1
for x in range(n, 0, -1):
    if is_valid(x):
        print(x)

## Task:

You have to write a function `pattern` which returns the following Pattern(See Examples) upto desired number of rows. 

* Note:```Returning``` the pattern is not the same as ```Printing``` the pattern.

### Parameters:
    
    pattern(        n        ,        x        ,        y        );
                    ^                 ^                 ^   
                    |                 |                 |
             Term upto which   Number of times   Number of times
              Basic Pattern     Basic Pattern     Basic Pattern
                should be         should be         should be
                 created          repeated           repeated
                                horizontally        vertically
    
* Note: `Basic Pattern` means what we created in Complete The Pattern #12

## Rules/Note:

* The pattern should be created using only unit digits.
* If `n < 1` then it should return "" i.e. empty string.
* If `x <= 1` then the basic pattern should not be repeated horizontally.
* If `y <= 1` then the basic pattern should not be repeated vertically.
* `The length of each line is same`, and is equal to the length of longest line in the pattern.
* Range of Parameters (for the sake of CW Compiler) :
  + `n ∈ (-∞,25]`
  + `x ∈ (-∞,10]`
  + `y ∈ (-∞,10]`
* If only two arguments are passed then the function `pattern` should run as if `y <= 1`.
* If only one argument is passed then the function `pattern` should run as if `x <= 1` & `y <= 1`.
* The function `pattern` should work when extra arguments are passed, by ignoring the extra arguments.

- The horizontal repetition (x) creates multiple copies of the basic pattern
- The vertical repetition (y) creates multiple copies of the row pattern

1. First, determine the basic pattern for each row
2. Apply horizontal repetition (x) 
3. Apply vertical repetition (y) 
4. Ensure all rows have the same length

## Implementation Strategy

5. For the vertical pattern, we need to create y copies of the row

# Let me re-analyze the pattern(4,3,2) example:

# 1 1 1 1

# Let me implement properly:

def pattern(n, x=None, y=None):
    if n < 1:
        return ""
    
    # Set default values
    if x is None:
        x = 1
    if y is None:
        y = 1
    
    # Ensure positive values
    x_val = max(1, x)
    y_val = max(1, y)
    
    # Generate the row pattern for 1 to n, then mirror it
    rows = []
    
    # First half: ascending from 1 to n
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        digit = i % 10
        # Create the horizontal pattern - digit repeated x times
        row_parts = [str(digit)] * x_val
        row = " ".join(row_parts)
        rows.append(row)
    
    # Second half: descending from n-1 to 1 (if n > 1)
    if n > 1:
        for i in range(n - 1, 0, -1):
            digit = i % 10
            row_parts = [str(digit)] * x_val
            row = " ".join(row_parts)
            rows.append(row)
    
    # Now repeat vertically y times
    result = []
    for _ in range(y_val):
        for row in rows:
            result.append(row)
    
    return "\n".join(result)

```python
def pattern(n, x=None, y=None):
    if n < 1:
        return ""
    
    # Set default values for x and y
    if x is None:
        x = 1
    if y is None:
        y = 1
    
    # Ensure positive values
    x_val = max(1, x)
    y_val = max(1, y)
    
    # Generate the base rows (1 to n, then back down to 1)
    rows = []
    
    # Ascending from 1 to n
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        digit = i % 10
        row_parts = [str(digit)] * x_val
        row = " ".join(row_parts)
        rows.append(row)
    
    # Descending from n-1 to 1 (only if we have more than one row)
    if n > 1:
        for i in range(n - 1, 0, -1):
            digit = i % 10
            row_parts = [str(digit)] * x_val
            row = " ".join(row_parts)
            rows.append(row)
    
    # Repeat the entire pattern vertically y times
    final_result = []
    for _ in range(y_val):
        for row in rows:
            final_result.append(row)
    
    return "\n".join(final_result)
```

Actually, I think I'm overcomplicating this. Let me look at the examples more carefully:

For `pattern(4,3,2)`, the pattern shows:
- Row 1: "1     1     1     1"
- Row 2: " 2   2 2   2 2   2 "

```python
def pattern(n, x=None, y=None):
    # Handle edge case for n < 1
    if n < 1:
        return ""
    
    # Determine actual x and y values based on argument count
    if x is None and y is None:
        x = 1
        y = 1
    elif y is None:
        y = 1
    elif x is None:
        x = 1
    
    # Ensure x and y are properly set
    x = x if x is not None else 1
    y = y if y is not None else 1
    
    # Create the pattern
    result = []
    
    # First part: from 1 to n (upward)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        row = ""
        # For each repetition horizontally
        for j in range(x):
            # Determine the spacing based on row index
            spacing = " " * (n - i)
            row += spacing + str(i)
        result.append(row)
    
    # Second part: from n-1 down to 1 (downward)
    for i in range(n - 1, 0, -1):
        row = ""
        for j in range(x):
            spacing = " " * (n - i)
            row += spacing + str(i)
        result.append(row)
    
    # Repeat vertically y times
    final_result = []
    for _ in range(y):
        for row in result:
            final_result.append(row)
    
    return "\n".join(final_result)
```

Wait, this is still not quite right. Looking at the examples more carefully:

- The number i is repeated x times with spacing

But looking at the exact examples, I see that "2 2" appears instead of just "2" in the second row.

- For row i, we have the number i
- This number appears x times with appropriate spacing that increases as we go down

```python
def pattern(n, x=None, y=None):
    # Handle edge case for n < 1
    if n < 1:
        return ""
    
    # Determine actual x and y values based on argument count
    if x is None and y is None:
        x = 1
        y = 1
    elif y is None:
        y = 1
    elif x is None:
        x = 1
    
    x = x if x is not None else 1
    y = y if y is not None else 1
    
    # Create the base pattern
    base_pattern = []
    
    # First half: upward from 1 to n
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        row = ""
        for j in range(x):
            # For each repetition of the pattern horizontally
            # The number i appears with decreasing spacing
            spacing = " " * (n - i)
            row += spacing + str(i)
        base_pattern.append(row)
    
    # Second half: downward from n-1 to 1
    for i in range(n - 1, 0, -1):
        row = ""
        for j in range(x):
            spacing = " " * (n - i)
            row += spacing + str(i)
        base_pattern.append(row)
    
    # Repeat the entire pattern vertically y times
    result = []
    for _ in range(y):
        for row in base_pattern:
            result.append(row)
    
    return "\n".join(result)
```

def pattern(n, x=None, y=None):
    # Handle edge case for n < 1
    if n < 1:
        return ""
    
    # Determine actual x and y values based on argument count
    if x is None and y is None:
        x = 1
        y = 1
    elif y is None:
        y = 1
    elif x is None:
        x = 1
    
    x = x if x is not None else 1
    y = y if y is not None else 1
    
    # Create the base pattern
    base_pattern = []
    
    # First half: upward from 1 to n
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        row = ""
        for j in range(x):
            # For each repetition of the pattern horizontally
            # The number i appears with decreasing spacing
            spacing = " " * (n - i)
            row += spacing + str(i)
        base_pattern.append(row)
    
    # Second half: downward from n-1 to 1
    for i in range(n - 1, 0, -1):
        row = ""
        for j in range(x):
            spacing = " " * (n - i)
            row += spacing + str(i)
        base_pattern.append(row)
    
    # Repeat the entire pattern vertically y times
    result = []
    for _ in range(y):
        for row in base_pattern:
            result.append(row)
    
    return "\n".join(result)
